The concept of null in general spaces and contexts
Suddhasattwa Das

Abstract
The notion of null sets, or nullity, is present in all discourses of mathematics. The two most familiar notions of nullity are ”almost-every” and ”almost none”. A notion of nullity corresponds to a choice of subsets that one interprets as null or empty. The rationale behind this choice depends on the context, such as Topology or Measure theory. One also expects that the morphisms or transformations within the contexts preserve the nullity structures. Extending this idea, a generalized notion of nullity is presented as a functor between categories. A constructive procedure is presented for by which an existing notion of nullity can be extended functorially to to categories with richer structure. Nullity is thus presented as an arbitrary construct, which can be extended to broader contexts using well defined rules. These rules are succinctly expressed by right and left Kan extensions.



Key words.
Category, Functor, Arrow category, Null sets

AMS subject classifications.
18D99, 18A25

1  Introduction

All discourses in Mathematics are rooted in some space, which is a set along with structure. For example the basic Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
could be studied as a vector space if the focus is on their linear structure, as a manifold if the focus is on their differential structure, or as metric spaces if the focus is on their in-built notion of distance. The mathematical properties to be examined are dictated by the choice of structure. Any mathematical property based on set theory automatically becomes synonymous with some subset of the space. The subset is simply the collection of points in the space which display the said property. It is called the
characteristic set
of that property. One is often faced with the question of whether a property is typical or common, which is interpreted as a question of the typicality of its characteristic set. Thus the simpler question is whether in a given space, a subset is empty, almost empty, almost full or full has been of fundamental importance throughout the development of mathematics. The article addresses the task of determining the true nature of the phrases
almost empty
and
almost full
.

The two most familiar notions of nullity are based on topology and measure respectively. If the space is a topological space, then its structure is borne in the collection of all neighborhoods and their preorder structure. This structure is used to establish the notions of ”proximity” and ”convergence”. A set
S
is called (topologically)-dense if it intersects each and every open set of the topology. This essentially means that the set
A
is arbitrarily near each and every point in the space. A set is then defined to be nowhere dense if its complement is dense.

Topological density is a powerful notion, and is the very basis of entire fields such as Learning theory. The essential task of Learning is to find a function that fits inputs to outputs. The candidates function could be from a general space
\mathcal{V}
but are searched from a restricted subset
\mathcal{H}
, called the
hypothesis space
. This task gets a firm mathematical footing by requiring that
\mathcal{H}
be dense within
\mathcal{V}
. In that case
\mathcal{H}
is said to be a
universal
approximator. There is a huge variety of hypothesis spaces
(
Das2023conditional
;
Das2024drift
;
DasGiannakis_RKHS_2018
;
DasDimitris_CascadeRKHS_2019
;
Das2023Lie
, e.g.)
suited for different contexts, all validated by the condition that they are universal approximators.

Topological density also arises in spaces with more complicated nature, for example the space of dynamical systems. Dynamical systems represent systems evolving under a deterministic rule of transformation. In spite of the huge variety of phenomenon that can be seen, their study is made easier by the fact that there are some canonical dynamical systems
(
DGJ_compactV_2018
;
BDWY2020
;
Das2023Koop_susp
;
Jewett1970prevalence
, e.g.)
which are dense topologically. This yet another intellectual merit of the notion of topological density, it justifies the study of some special cases, if they are topologically dense.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Affine embeddings}\\
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Collection of}\\
\mbox{set-maps into}V\end{array}\right\}}}

\mbox{s.t. }\pi(T)=A\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Nullity induced}\\
\mbox{by set-map}T\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Linear embeddings}\\
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\Null(A,V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\mbox{Vector field }V\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\Null(V)}}
projection
projection
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Intersection / min optimization}\\
\mbox{over all affine T s.t. }\pi(T)=A\end{array}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Union / max optimization}\\
\mbox{over all embeddings }A\end{array}}

Figure 1
:
Construction of prevalence. The notion of prevalence, formulated in
HSYprevalence1992
is one of the main inspirations for the analysis in this article. Prevalence is an extension of the notion of Lebesgue-null to an infinite dimensional Euclidean spaces, to infinite dimensional vector spaces. Thus prevalence assigns to any vector space
V
a collection of sets labeled
\Null(V)
which is closed under subsets, and which coincides with the usual Lebesgue zero measure sets if
V
is finite dimensional. The construction of
\Null(V)
goes through several steps, as presented in (
1
) and (
4
). One first takes an arbitrary affine embedding of some
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into
V
, and obtains a push-forward of the null sets on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Next one takes the intersection of all these collections, as
T
is varied with its linear part
A
kept fixed. This leads to an
A
-dependent collection
\Null(A,V)
of null-sets. Finally, one takes a union of all these
\Null(A,V)
for various
A
s, to get the final nullity structure
\Null(V)
. This article shows how these steps are categorical in nature, and leads to a notion of nullity which is both invariant and an extension. The categorical approach allows this construction to be applied to several other contexts as well.

This useful notion of topological density is inadequate for many other situations. In the study of dynamical systems, one sees a peculiar feature in chaotic systems – periodic points are topologically dense but statistically null
(
DasYorke2020
;
DasSaddles2015
;
DasJim17_chaos
, e.g.)
. An even more simpler example is the density of rational numbers on the real line. A number picked at number is almost surely irrational. Thus topological full-ness might be irrelevant from a statistical or measure theoretic point of view.

This prompts the formulation of the second notion of fullness or emptiness – in terms of measure. One of the major breakthroughs in physics and mathematics was KAM theory
(
Arnold1965
;
Arnold1963small
;
Herman1
;
Das2018Tongue
, e.g.)
. It relies on the realization that the key property (quasiperiodicity) required to make conclusions about toral dynamics may not be universal but is measure theoretically full.

The two notions – Topological density and Measure theoretically full, are adequate for a lot of mathematical discussions. They get interconnected whenever the reference measure in non-zero on the open sets of the topology. This is the case for the Haar measure on finite dimensional Lie groups. In such cases, being measure theoretically full implies being topologically dense. This is a simple and useful connection between two notions which are otherwise independent. The problem is that there is no natural Haar measure for infinite dimensional Lie groups, such as infinite dimensional vector spaces.

The solution to this problem was the notion of
prevalence

OttYorke_prevalence_2005
;
HSYprevalence1992
. Let
\mathcal{V}
be an infinite dimensional vector space, and
S
a subset. Then
S
is said to be
shy
is there is a finite dimensional vector subspace
P
called
probe
such that

\Leb_{P}\left(P\cap\left\{S+v\right\}=0\right),\quad\forall v\in\cal V.

(1)

A subset is said to be
prevalent
if its complement is shy. Thus shy and prevalent are analogs of almost empty and almost full, for infinite dimensional vector spaces. These notions have enabled results of fundamental importance to be developed
(
SauerEtAl1991
;
HuntKaloshin1997proj
;
sontag2003differential
;
fraysse2006smooth
;
HuntKaloshin1999reg
;
Kaloshin1997prevalence
;
Hunt1994dffrntl
, e.g.)
. Note that according to (
1
), if a set
A
is prevalent or shy, then so is any translate
A+v
of
A
. This translation-invariance makes prevalence a natural definition for vector spaces.

Corollary 1
.

The notion of prevalence is assigns a collection of null-sets to every vector space
V
. The collection remains invariant under translations within
V
. The collection coincides with the collection of zero-Lebesgue measure sets when
V
is finite dimensional.

In spite of the naturality of the existing definition of prevalence, it is still inadequate to describe typical behavior in many situations. As pointed out in
BerryDas2023learning
;
BerryDas2024review
, the existing notions of prevalence is not adequate for describing nonlinear phenomenon. In skew product dynamical systems
(
MustaveeEtAl_covid_2021
;
DasMustAgar2023_qpd
;
Stark1999
, e.g.)
which take the form

\begin{split}x_{n+1}&=f_{X}(x_{n})\\
y_{n+1}&=f_{Y}(x_{n},y_{n})\end{split},

(2)

The dynamics in the
x
-variable is autonomous and drives the dynamics of the
y
-variable. Under various conditions the driving dynamics
f_{X}
is embedded into the driven dynamical system
f_{Y}
, and it is an open question as to whether this embedding is typical. This question has deep implications in learning theory and control theory. The existing notions of prevalence is built upon linearity, and cannot capture the highly nonlinear and non-explicit nature of the correspondence between the
x
- and
y
- variables of (
2
).

Typicality and Nullity are two sides of the same coin. Nullity is thus not a fixed and pre-conceived notion in spaces, but often needs to be adapted. Ideally when one conceives of a notion of nullity, that is, declares that a certain collection of sets in a space
V
are the null sets, some logical rules are follows. For example,

1.

subsets of a null set must be null too;

2.

if
V
belongs to a class of similar objects, then the transformations or relations that bind these objects must preserve the respective nullity structures;

3.

if
V
contains imprints or embeddings of structures such as Euclidean spaces, its new nullity structure should be an extension of those on the embedded images.

The goal of this article is to present a very general construction of nullity, that preserves these properties.

The abstraction and generalization of nullity that we undertake is inspired by Sauer, Hunt and Yorke’s construction of prevalence
HSYprevalence1992
. The essential feature of prevalence are certain structural properties which are not limited to vector fields. These structural properties encode the embedding of the category of vector spaces within the category of affine spaces, and the projection from the latter to the former. We axiomatize such a relation in categorical language, in Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
.

The categorical reconstruction of nullity also provides a separation of the ideas that go into nullity - there are some which are purely set-theoretic and some which are dependent on the context, which for example, could be vector spaces or manifolds. The former are universal and used in all construct of nullity. One of the contributions of this article is the category of
\Nullity
, which provides a concise mathematical definition of the universal set theoretic aspects of nullity.

Outline

We next take a closer look at the construction of prevalence in Section
2
. The definition will be restated in a manner that makes it suitable for immediate generalization. Next in Section
3
we present a general and broad definition of nullity, that uses the language of categories and functors. This allows a discussion of nullity which is very context independent. The categorical language developed is used to obtain a categorical redefinition of nullity and prevalence in Section
4
. Finally in Section
5
we analyze properties such as uniqueness, invariance and extendability of the nullity constructs. Section
6
contain some technical results and proofs.

2  Deconstructing prevalence

We now take a closer look at the concept of prevalence. Henceforth we shall use the term
null
synonymously with
shy
. We restate (
1
) as

S\mbox{ null}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Leb_{P}\left\{p\in P\;:\;p\in\left\{S+v\right\}\right\}=0,\;\forall v\in\cal V.

The translate
v
is an invertible affine transform. Thus the above condition may be re-written as

S\mbox{ null}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Leb_{P}\left\{p\in P\;:\;p+v\in S\right\}=0,\quad;\forall v\in\cal V.

Note that a probe is a finite dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{V}
. The subspace
P
may be interpreted as the image of a linear embedding
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V
. For each
v\in V
,
x\mapsto Ax+v
is an affine map from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V
. Given two vector spaces
X,Y
let
\Affine(X;Y)
denote the set of affine linear maps from
X
to
Y
. Then the condition may be re-written as

S\mbox{ null}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}T^{-1}(S)=0,\;\forall T\in\Affine\left(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{V}\right),\;\text{Lin}(T)\equiv A.

(3)

Equation (
3
) restates (
1
) in terms of inverse image under maps. A class of maps has been identified as
\Affine(X;Y)
and nullity is in terms of inverse images under these maps. Let
\left[\text{Euc}\right]
denote the collection of finite dimensional vector spaces. Given an
X\in\left[\text{Euc}\right]
let
\Null(X)
denote the subsets of
X
which are null with respect to Lebesgue measure of
X
, which we have set as the natural measure for
X
. Let
\Affine\left(\mathbb{R}^{d};V\right)
denote the collection of affine maps from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
V
. Define

\Null\left(V;T\right):=\left\{\mathcal{S}\in 2^{V}\;:\;T^{-1}(\mathcal{S})\in\Null\left(\dom(T)\right)\right\},\quad T\in\Affine\left(\mathbb{R}^{d};V\right),\;v\in V.

For every linear map
A
and vector
v
in its codomain, let
T_{A,v}
denote the affine map
x\mapsto Ax+v
. We fix the notation
\Null\left(V;A,v\right):=\Null\left(V;T_{A,v}\right)
. The construction of prevalence follows the sequence :

\Null\left(V;A\right):=\cap\left\{\Null\left(V;A,v\right)\;:\;v\in V\right\},\quad\Null\left(V\right):=\cup\left\{\Null\left(V;A\right)\;:\;A\in\Hom_{\text{Lin},\text{mono}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d};V\right)\right\}.

In summary, the collection of null sets in
V
is defined by the following max-min optimization :

\boxed{\Null(V)=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Linear embedding}\\
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V\\
\end{array}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cap}}{{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Affine embedding}\\
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V\\
\proj(T)=A\end{array}}}\begin{array}[]{c}\Null\left(V;T\right)\\
\\
\\
\end{array}}

(4)

The construction (
4
) while being equivalent to (
1
) presents our approach to the question of nullity and genericity. Now the shy or null sets in an infinite dimensional space
\mathcal{V}
is in terms of the null sets of the finite dimensional spaces. This makes nullity a structural concept. Figure
1
deconstructs this definition into logical steps. Nullity thus has the following elements to it :

1.

An infinite collection
\mathcal{C}
- which in this case is the collection of all vector spaces, finite and infinite.

2.

A concept of nullity for some basic objects of this collection - in this case the finite dimensional vector spaces.

3.

Nullity is a label attached to pairs
(S,X)
, with
X
being an object of
\mathcal{C}
and
S
a subset of
X
.

4.

The collection of objects in
\mathcal{C}
are bound to each other by some select relations - in this case affine maps.

5.

Nullity in a general object
X
is in terms of pull-backs along these relations.

All of this suggest categorical structures being present in all stages.

Category

The arrangement
\mathcal{C}
has objects and relations which can be composed. The composition of two affine maps is again affine. All this points to the mathematical property of a
category
and nullity as a categorical or compositional property. A category is a bare structural definition that may be found in contexts of very different kinds. We present our categorical approach in the next section. The categorical approach is the logical choice for making a generalization of the notions of prevalent / typical, null / shy, in context different from linear spaces and affine maps, such as to differential maps and manifolds.

A category
\mathcal{C}
is a collection of two kinds of entities :

1.

objects : usually representing different instances of the same mathematical construct;

2.

morphism : connecting arrows from one object to another; which satisfy the following three properties –

3.

compositionality : given any three objects
a,b,c
of
\mathcal{C}
and two morphisms
a\xrightarrow{f}b
and
b\xrightarrow{g}c
, the morphisms can be joined end-to-end to create a composite morphism represented as
a\xrightarrow{g\circ f}b
;

4.

associativity : the composition of morphisms is associative;

5.

identity morphism : each object
a
is endowed with a morphism
\Id_{x}
called the
identity
morphism, which play the role of unit element in composition.

Given two points
x,y
in the object set
ob(\mathcal{C})
, the collection of arrows from
x
to
y
is denoted as
\Hom(x;y)
. Note that this collection may be infinite, finite or even empty. The last criterion implies that for each
x

\Hom(x;x)
has at least one member. Whenever there are multiple categories being discussed, one uses the notations
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x;y)
or
\mathcal{C}(x;y)
to indicate that the morphisms are within the category
\mathcal{C}
.

Examples of categories

One of the most fundamental categories is
\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
, the category in which objects are sets up to a certain prefixed cardinality, and arrows are arbitrary maps. Similarly
\Topo
denotes the category of topological spaces, with continuous maps as arrows. We denote by
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
the category in which the objects are vector spaces and arrows are linear maps. The collection
\Affine
that we have already defined has the same objects as
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
but all affine maps as morphisms. Note that this includes the morphisms in
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
. This makes
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
a
subcategory
of
\Affine
. Suppose
\mathcal{U}
is any set. Then the power-set
2^{\mathcal{U}}
of subsets of
\mathcal{U}
is a category, in which the relations are the subset
\subseteq
relations. Note that there can be only at most one arrow between any two objects
A,B
of this category, which is to be interpreted as inclusion. Such categories are known as
preorders
, and other examples are the category of ordered natural numbers, real numbers, open covers, and the concept of infinitesimal
(
Das2023CatEntropy
, see)
. Note that the usual notion of prevalence (
1
), (
4
) is about objects and morphisms in the category
\Affine
. However, the label itself applies to arbitrary subsets of a vector space, a relation non-existent within
\Affine
. The subset relation is contained within some suitable chosen power set category. The notion of an inverse image also involves the category
\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
, which is thus a third category that gets involved in the definition. To be able to work simultaneously with different categories and relate one with another, we need the notion of transformations that preserve categorical structure :
functor
s.

Functor

Given two categories
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
, a functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a mapping between their objects along with the following properties :

1.

For each
x,y\in ob(\mathcal{C})
, there is an induced map
F:\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x;y)\to\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(Fx;Fy)
. Thus arrows / morphisms between any pair of points get mapped into arrows between the corresponding pair of points in the image.

2.

F
preserves compositionality : given any three objects
a,b,c
of
\mathcal{C}
and two morphisms
a\xrightarrow{f}b
and
b\xrightarrow{g}c
,
F(g\circ f)=F(g)\circ F(f)
.

3.

F
preserves identity :
F(\Id_{a})=\Id_{F(a)}
.

In summary, a functor is a map between the object-sets that also preserves the underlying categorical structure. Categorical structure is essentially compositionality. This preservation property is expressed through the last two criterion. The language of categories and functors have helped create a
synthetic
approach to a wide array of topics, such as homology, set theory, Lebesgue integration, limits and fractals
(
Das2024hmlgy
;
Das2024cells
;
Das2024slice
;
Adamek2018colimit
;
Ulmer1968
, e.g.)
. We are now ready to begin a categorical redefinition of nullity in multiple contexts.

3  Set theoretic aspects of nullity

Nullity is essentially a set-theoretic concept. Regardless of the context such as manifolds or vector spaces, the collection of null sets lies in the realm of sets. Recall that :

Definition 1

(Down-set)
.

Given a preorder
\mathcal{O}
, a down-set is a complete sub-preorder, i.e., a collection
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
of objects of
\mathcal{O}
such that if
b\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
,
a
is an object in
\mathcal{O}
and
a\leq b
, then
a
belongs in
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
too.

The most common example of a preorder is the power set
2^{V}
of the underlying space
V
. Definition
1
is essential since the concept of nullity is in fact a choice of a down-set within
2^{V}
. This collection is closed under unions, intersections and contains the empty set. This is a subcategory of the power-set preorder, which has all limits, an initial element, and countable coproducts.

Definition 2

(Nullity for sets)
.

Given a set
A
, a nullity structure or concept of nullity for
A
is a down-set of the power-set
2^{A}
of
A
.

Nullity is primarily a set theoretic aspect. We now extend it to objects in arbitrary categories. The following will be a standing assumption throughout the discussion :

Assumption 1
.

There is a category
\mathcal{M}
, to be interpreted as the main category, and a functor
\gamma:\mathcal{M}\to\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
.

The functor
\gamma
acts as the bridge from
\mathcal{M}
to
\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
. This allows the definition :

Definition 3

(Nullity for individual objects)
.

A nullity-structure for an object
V
in the category
\mathcal{M}
that satisfies Assumption
1
, is a nullity structure for the set
\gamma(V)
.

The next category enables a concise and categorical definition of the set-theoretic aspect of nullity.

Definition 4

(Nullity category)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a collection of sets. Then
\Nullity(\mathcal{S})
denotes the category whose objects are

\left(A,\mathcal{N}_{A}\right)\;:\;A\in\mathcal{S},\,\mathcal{N}_{A}\mbox{ is a nullity structure of }A.

A morphism
\phi
from an object
\left(A,\mathcal{N}_{A}\right)
into an object
\left(B,\mathcal{N}_{B}\right)
corresponds to a map
\phi:A\to B
such that

\phi(A)\in\mathcal{N}_{B},\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{N}_{A}.

(5)

It is routine to check compositionality and associativity in this category. The rule in (
5
) upholds the principle that a null set cannot be transformed into a non-null set, it must be transformed into another null set. The collection
\mathcal{S}
is often chosen to be the collection of all sets up to a certain cardinality. In that case
\mathcal{S}
is dropped from the notation and we just use
\Nullity
for simplicity. An alternate way of defining morphisms is as set-theoretic maps
\phi:A\to B
such that

\phi^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{N}_{A},\quad\forall B\in\mathcal{N}_{A}B.

This rule upholds the principle that a non-null set cannot be mapped into a null set. However the resulting categorical structure would not be conducive to our analysis. There is an obvious forgetful functor

\bot:\Nullity(\mathcal{S})\to\left[\text{Set}(\mathcal{S})\right],

with
\left[\text{Set}(\mathcal{S})\right]
being the sub-category of
\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket
spanned by the sets in the collection
\mathcal{S}
. The true role of the functorial nature of
\gamma
is brought to light from the next definition :

Definition 5

(Nullity for categories)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a category satisfying Assumption
1
. Then a nullity-structure for
\mathcal{B}
is a functor
\Null:\mathcal{B}\to\Nullity
such that the following commutation holds

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}

(6)

Thus a nullity construct for the category
\mathcal{M}
associates to each object
m\in\mathcal{M}
the set
\gamma(m)
along with a down-set
\mathcal{N}_{m}
of the power set of
\gamma(m)
. This assignment must be such that for every morphism
f:m\to m^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{M}
, the following rule is observed :

\gamma(f)(A)\in\mathcal{N}_{m^{\prime}},\quad\forall A\in\mathcal{N}_{m}.

(7)

Example 1

(Lebesgue nullity)
.

Let
\left[\text{Euc}\right]
be the category of finite vector spaces, and linear maps as morphisms. Let
\left[\text{Euc}\right]_{\text{mono}}
denote the sub-category in which the morphisms are restricted to injective maps. Then the assignment of each finite dimensional space to its collection of Lebesgue zero-measure sets, is a nullity-construct in the sense of Definition
5
.

Example 2

(Nowhere dense)
.

Let
\left[\text{Man}^{1}\right]
be the category of manifolds and
C^{1}
-differentiable maps. Then the assignment of each manifold to its collection of nowhere dense sets, is a nullity-construct in the sense of Definition
5
.

Example 3

(Measure spaces)
.

Let
\left[\text{Meas}\right]
be the category of measurable spaces, and a morphism between two measure spaces
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
and
(\Omega^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a map
f:\Omega\to\Omega^{\prime}
which is measurable with respect to
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}
and such that
f_{*}\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu^{\prime}
. The assignment to each
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
the collection of sets in
\Sigma
which have
\mu
-measure zero, is a nullity-construct in the sense of Definition
5
.

For the next example, recall that a
G-\delta
set is a countable intersection of open sets. A complement of a
G-\delta
set is called an
F-\sigma
set.

Example 4

(F-sigma)
.

Let
\Topo
be the category of topological spaces and continuous maps. The assignment to each topological space
\Omega
its collection of
F-\sigma
sets, is a nullity-construct in the sense of Definition
5
.

To complete our understanding of
\Nullity
we recall one final general categorical concept.

Comma categories

A general arrangement of categories and functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

creates a special category called a
comma category

\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
. Its objects are

ob\left(\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]\right):=\left\{\left(a,b,\phi\right)\;:\;a\in ob(\mathcal{A}),\,b\in ob(\mathcal{B}),\,\phi\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}\left(\alpha a;\beta b\right)\right\},

and the morphisms comprise of pairs

\left\{(f,g)\;:\;f\in\Hom(D),\,g\in\Hom(E)\right\}
such that the following commutation holds :

This category
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
may be interpreted as connections between the functors
\alpha,\beta
, via their common codomain
\mathcal{C}
. Comma categories contain as sub-structures, the original categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, via the
forgetful
functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}

whose action on morphisms in
\left[\alpha\;;\;\beta\right]
can be described as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\alpha a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\alpha a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\beta b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\beta b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}b}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Comma categories prevail all over category theory and mathematics. If either
\mathcal{A}
or
\mathcal{B}
is taken to be
\star
the trivial category with a single object , then the resulting comma categories are called left and right
slice-categories
respectively. If
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{C}
, then the comma category becomes the
arrow-category
. The objects here are the arrows in
\mathcal{C}
, and the morphisms are commutation squares. Comma, slice and arrow categories thus represent finer structures present within categories. Comma categories are used to represent various compound objects in mathematics
(
Das2023CatEntropy
;
DasSuda2024recon
;
DasSuda2025enrich
, e.g.)
. The objects of a comma category are essentially morphisms, with their domain and codomain sourced from different categories. We next see how nullity from a component of a comma category leads to nullity for the entire comma category.

Nullity for commas

We now show that if Assumption
2
holds, then the concept of nullity can be extended. Suppose the arrangement shown below on the left holds :

\mathcal{X}
and

in the following manner :

(9)

We next begin the categorical axiomatization of nullity. The construction in (
8
) will be indispensable in this analysis.

4  Categorical axiomatization

The construction starts with the assumption

Assumption 2
.

There is a category
\mathcal{B}
to be interpreted as the base-category, equipped with a notion of nullity, i.e., a functor
\Null:\mathcal{B}\to\Nullity
.

The construction of nullity has a starting arrangement
\mathcal{B}
(finite dimensional Euclidean spaces) with an existing notion of nullity (Lebesgue nullity). There is also a final arrangement
\mathcal{M}
(infinite dimensional affine spaces) to which the notion of nullity has been extended to. For that purpose, an intermediate arrangement
\mathcal{I}
(infinite dimensional vector spaces) is required, by which the
\mathcal{B}
-objects can be compared to the
\mathcal{M}
-objects. The arrangement
\mathcal{I}
has the same object complexity as
\mathcal{M}
, but less morphism complexity. They provide a common space in which
\mathcal{M}
objects may be paired with
\mathcal{B}
objects. These pairings are precisely the probes. The next two assumption formalizes this :

Assumption 3
.

There are two categories
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{M}
, to be interpreted as an intermediate category and the main category, along with functors creating the following arrangement :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(a)

Abstract categories and functors. This is the content of Assumption
3
.

(b)

Euclidean spaces, Vector spaces and Affine spaces.
\left[\text{Euc}\right]_{\text{mono}}
,
\left[\text{Vec}\right]_{\text{mono}}
and
\left[\text{Aff}\right]_{\text{mono}}
are respectively the categories of finite vector spaces with injective linear maps, vector spaces with injective linear maps, and vector spaces with injective affine maps. See Example
5
.

Intermediate and main category same as base category. The special case of Sub-figure (a) in which

leads to an expansion of any existing nullity structure on
\mathcal{B}
. See Definition

for more details.

.

.

(f)

Let
\left[\text{Man}^{k+1}\right]_{\text{submer},pt}
be the category whose objects are pairs
(M,x)
of a
C^{k+1}
manifold
M
and a point
x
on it. A morphism between two such objects are
C^{k+1}
submersion maps which preserve the points.
\left[\text{Jet}^{k}\text{Euc}_{0}\right]_{\text{submer}}
is the sub-category of
\left[\text{Jet}^{k}\text{Euc}_{0}\right]
in which the morphisms are restricted to those having an invertible first derivative. See Example
8

Figure 2
:
Instances of (
10
). One of our main assumptions is the arrangement of categories of functors presented in the pattern of (
10
). The panels show several instances of this pattern. The pattern provides a means of lifting a nullity structure from the base category
\mathcal{B}
to the main category
\mathcal{M}
. Note that these diagrams are independent of the existing nullity structure on
\mathcal{B}
, presented in (
6
). A construction of nullity thus depends on independent choices of (
6
) and (
10
).

Figure
2
presents several instances of (
10
). All of these examples display the same structure expressed in the categorical diagram. With this in mind we now re-examine the construction of prevalence, using the language of categories and functors. Now all the conclusions about the setup in Figure
2(b)
will also hold for any setup satisfying Assumptions
1
–
3
.

1.

Consider any vector space
V
. It is an object of both
\Affine
as well as
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
. We choose the former.

2.

A linear embedding
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V
corresponds to an object of the left-slice category :

\left[\hbox to154.11pt{\vbox to38.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 77.05472pt\lower-19.39177pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-23.59811pt}{-26.95695pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The top and bottom rows correspond to two comma categories. Such a commutation leads to a functor between the comma categories, displayed as the unlabeled arrow in the diagram on the right. The bottom horizontal arrow is created using the construction in (
8
). The composition of these two functors leads to a nullity structure on
\left[j_{1}j_{2}\;;\;\mathcal{M}\right]
.

We now have all the ingredients for redefining the construction of prevalence as presented in (
4
) and Figure
1
. The construction is done using a powerful tool from Category theory.

Kan extensions

Kan extensions
(
perrone2022kan
;
street2004categorical
;
Riehl_homotopy_2014
, e.g.)
are universal constructions which generalize the practice of taking partial minima or maxima, in a functorial manner.Consider the following arrangement :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}

(12)

of functors and categories. A
left Kan extension
or
right envelope
of
F
along
K
is a functor
\psi:D\to E
along with a minimum natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow\psi\circ K
. With a slight departure from usual convention, we denote this functor
\psi
as
\mathcal{RE}_{K}\left(F\right)
. This pair
\left(\mathcal{RE}_{K}\left(F\right),\eta\right)
is also minimum / universal in the sense that for every other functor
H:D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\gamma:F\Rightarrow H\circ K
, there is a natural transformation
\tilde{\gamma}:\mathcal{RE}_{K}\left(F\right)\Rightarrow H
s.t.
\gamma=\left(\tilde{\gamma}\star\Id_{K}\right)\circ\eta
. This is shown in the diagram below.

One can similarly define a
right Kan extension
or
left-envelope
of
F
along
K
. It is a functor
\mathcal{LE}_{K}\left(F\right):D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\epsilon:\mathcal{LE}_{K}\left(F\right)\circ K\Rightarrow F
. Moreover, this pair
\left(\mathcal{LE}_{K}\left(F\right),\epsilon\right)
is maximum / universal in the sense that for every other functor
H:D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\gamma:H\circ K\Rightarrow F
, there is a natural transformation
\tilde{\gamma}:H\Rightarrow\mathcal{LE}_{K}\left(H\right)
such that
\gamma=\epsilon\circ\left(\tilde{K}\star\Id_{K}\right)
. This is shown in the diagram below.

The act of finding limits or colimits is analogous to finding the minimum or maximum under this constraint. Many constructions in mathematics which are analogous to constrained optimizations, can be succinctly expressed in the language of Kan extensions.

Max-min optimization

Using the language of Kan extensions, we make the following Kan extensions using the functors
\pi_{*}
and
\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}
constructed above :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[j_{1}j_{2}\;;\;\mathcal{M}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[j_{2}\;;\;\pi\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{probed}-\Null}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{LE}_{\pi_{*}}\left(\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{RE}_{\bot_{2}}\left(\mathcal{LE}_{\pi_{*}}\left(\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}\right)\right)}

(13)

The middle arrow in yellow, achieves the
\mathcal{M}
-invariance of nullity by virtue of being a right-envelope (i.e. left Kan extension). However it is not a nullity structure on
\mathcal{M}
itself, but on morphisms sourced from
\mathcal{B}
-objects via
\mathcal{I}
. Borrowing the terminology from
HSYprevalence1992
, we call such a morphism a
probe
. Thus this notion of nullity is tied to a choice of a probe object, and we call this a
probed notion of nullity
. The lowermost arrow in green, represents the construction of nullity for the main category
\mathcal{M}
. By virtue of being a left-envelope (i.e. right Kan extension), it is the union of all probed nullities. In other words, it is the minimal nullity structure that contains the nullity structure produced by all the probes. relates this abstract categorical construction to the construction of prevalence.

The extension of nullity was done solely using collection of morphisms available within the arrow category
\left[j_{2}\;;\;\pi\right]
. Thus although intuitively we interpret nullity as a pre-existing structure and nullity-preservation as a derived property, this categorical construction assumes the morphisms as a primitive notion, and optimizes the nullity structure that would be preserved. This is in principle similar to several approaches to categorification, such as pseudo-Borel spaces
HeunenEtAl2017cnvnt
,
Chen spaces
Chen1977iterated
and diffeological spaces
Stacey2008smooth
;
BaezHoffnung2011cnvnnt
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Linear embeddings}\\
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V\\
\mbox{and affine embeddings }\\
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V\\
\mbox{s.t. }\pi(T)=A\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[j_{1}j_{2}\;;\;\mathcal{M}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Nullity induced}\\
\mbox{by set-map}T\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Linear embeddings}\\
A:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V\end{array}\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[j_{2}\;;\;\pi\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\Null(A;V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\left\{\mbox{Vector field }V\right\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\Null(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{LE}_{\pi_{*}}\left(\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{RE}_{!V}\left(\mathcal{LE}_{\pi_{*}}\left(\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}\right)\right)}
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Intersection / min optimization}\\
\mbox{over all T s.t. }\pi(T)=A\end{array}}
{\color[rgb]{0.3,0.3,0.3}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{Union / max optimization}\\
\mbox{over all embeddings }A\end{array}}

Figure 3
:
Categorical explanation of prevalence. Figure
1
presented a flowchart outlining the construction the notion of prevalence (
4
). The categorical construction in (
13
) has been superimposed om that flowchart. The various collections in Figure
1
can now be related to various categories. The correspondences between the collections turn out to be functors. The construction of prevalence is done using a successive max / min optimizations. These optimization steps are shown to correspond to Kan extensions. This categorical interpretation completely dissociates the construction of [revalence] from the context to vectors spaces and affine maps. It therefore becomes applicable to any other context, that satisfies certain structural or categorical assumptions (
1
–
3
).

Example 5

(Prevalence)
.

As declared before, prevalence is the special case of (
13
) displayed in Figure
2(b)
. The base notion of nullity which is used is given in Example
1
. Figure
3
elaborates this connection.

Example 6

(Jets 1)
.

Let
\left[\text{Jet}^{k}\text{Euc}_{0}\right]
denote the category in which objects are Euclidean spaces, and the morphisms between two spaces
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are
C^{k+1}
maps
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
which map the origin to the origin, and with order
k
Taylor expansions. Figure
2(d)
elaborates this connection.

Example 7

(Jets 2)
.

Let
\left[\text{Jet}^{k}\text{Euc}_{0}\right]
denote the category in which objects are Euclidean spaces, and the morphisms between two spaces
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are
C^{k+1}
maps
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
which map the origin to the origin, and with order
k
Taylor expansions. Figure
2(e)
elaborates this connection.

Example 8

(Manifolds)
.

Figure
2(f)
presents how the nullity structure on
\left[\text{Jet}^{k}\text{Euc}_{0}\right]
can be used to create a nullity structure on the collection of pointed manifolds. This structure would be preserved by point preserving submersions between manifolds.

This completes a categorical redefinition of prevalence and shy sets, in an abstract categorical setting. The two ingredients are a pre-existing notion of nullity on
\mathcal{B}
, as declared in Assumption
2
. The other ingredient is the arrangement in (
10
), as claimed in Assumption
3
. A construction of nullity thus depends on independent choices of (
6
) and (
10
). Figure
2
presents several instances of these assumptions which lead to other notions of nullity. We next examine the mathematical consequences of the construction in (
13
).

5  Main results

The most trivial consequence of the categorical nature of our constructions is :

Theorem 2

(Invariance of Nullity)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. Then the nullity created for
\mathcal{M}
using the construction (
13
) is invariant under the endomorphisms of
\mathcal{M}
.

The invariance follows from our interpretation in Definition
5
of nullity as a functor, and the categorical structure of
\mathcal{M}
and
\Nullity
. The next important property to establish is uniqueness. Uniqueness can be established based on the following desirable property :

Definition 6

(Testability)
.

Let
\Null
be a nullity construct according to Definition
4
of an object
V
of
\mathcal{M}
. This nullity is said to be testable if there is a probe- object
\phi:j_{1}(b)\to V
in
\mathcal{I}
such that the push-forward of
\Null(b)
under
\phi
is a sub-structure of
\Null(V)
.

Theorem 3

(Nullity is unique)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. Let
\tilde{\Null}
be a nullity construct for objects of
\mathcal{M}
,s satisfying the following two criterion

1.

\tilde{\Null}
is preserved under morphisms in
\mathcal{M}
;

2.

\tilde{\Null}
is testable.

Then
\Null(V)
constructed from (
13
) is a sub-collection of
\tilde{\Null}(V)
.

Condition (i) of Theorem
3
is just the condition for functoriality. Condition (ii) can be interpreted as
\tilde{\Null}(V)
containing the probed nullity structure of some probe. Theorem
3
is thus a direct interpretation of the successive Kan extensions involved in the construction of Nullity. The next property to be expected from (
13
) is that the newly constructed nullity functor on
\mathcal{M}
be an extension of the existing nullity on
\mathcal{B}
. There is however an a basic obstruction to this happening.

Enriching nullity structure

Consider the particular case of Assumption
3
displayed in Figure
2(c)
. In that case (
13
) takes the form :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{B}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{B}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Null}}

(14)

Note that the induced map
\pi_{*}
is just an identity between comma categories. As a result, the first right envelope is just the original functor. The new creation is the functor
\overline{\Null}
. Its action on morphisms of
\mathcal{B}
can be formulated explicitly as

\begin{split}\overline{\Null}(A)&=\cup\left\{\Null_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}}(\phi)\;:\;\phi:A^{\prime}\to A\right\}\\
&=\cup\left\{a\subseteq\gamma(A)\;:\;\phi:A^{\prime}\to A,\,(\gamma\phi)^{-1}(a)\in\Null(A^{\prime})\right\}\end{split}

(15)

Note that
\overline{\Null}
is a superset of
\Null
. Take any
a\in\Null(A)
, and take
\phi
to be the identity
\Id_{A}:A\to A
. Then by (
15
)
a
lies in
\overline{\Null}(A)
too. In general one cannot expect
\overline{\Null}
to coincide with
\Null
.

Definition 7

(Saturated nullity structure)
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold. Then the nullity structure is said to be saturated if the functor
\overline{\Null}
coincides with
\Null
.

To prove our main result, we need an assumption based on the following inclusion functor :

(16)

The inclusion
\iota_{3}
formalizes the intuition that arrows in
\mathcal{B}
are also arrows in
\mathcal{I}
from a
\mathcal{B}
object to an
\mathcal{I}
-object. We need to assume

Assumption 4
.

The functor
\iota_{3}
has a post right inverse
\iota_{3}^{R*}:\left[j_{2}\;;\;\mathcal{I}\right]\to\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{B}\right]

Theorem 4

(Nullity is an extension)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold, and suppose that the nullity structure
\Null
on
\mathcal{B}
is saturated. Then for any object
V
from
\mathcal{B}
,
\Null(V)
coincides with
\Null(j_{1}j_{2}(B))
.

The proof depends on realizing certain functorial relations between comma categories. The commutative diagram on the left :

(18)

The proof can be summarized in the following large commutation diagram that involves these functors.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\gamma\;;\;\gamma\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\gamma j_{1}j_{2}\;;\;\gamma\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[j_{1}j_{2}\;;\;\mathcal{M}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{B}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[j_{2}\;;\;\pi\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Arrow}\left[\mathcal{B}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Nullity_{j_{1}j_{2},\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{LE}_{\pi_{*}}\left(\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Null}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{RE}_{\bot_{2}}\left(\mathcal{LE}_{\pi_{*}}\left(\Null_{\mathcal{I},\mathcal{B}}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Null}}

(19)

The dashed arrows represent the Kan extensions displayed in (
13
), while the dotted arrows represent Kan extension displayed in (
14
). The main message of this diagram is the commutation between the corresponding pair of Kan extensions. The lowermost commutation is precisely the extension claimed in Theorem
4
. Thus the proof requires a verification of (
19
). The following lemma will come useful :

Lemma 5.1
.

Let the same assumptions as Theorem
4
hold. Then :

1.

The functors
\pi_{*}
from (
11
) and
\bot_{2}
have right inverses.

2.

The functor
\iota_{2}
from (
18
) has a post right adjoint.

3.

The functor
\iota_{1}
from (
17
) and
\bot_{2}
has a pre- right adjoint.

Lemma
5.1
is proved in Section
6.5
. The proof of diagram (
19
) requires a total of 6 commutation relations to be verified.

1.

This first three are the three commutation squares along the left column of the figure

) are proved in Section

.

2.

Upper commuting triangle (yellow) : The two nullity structures are defined by composition. So their commutation holds due to the two commutations in the row above.

3.

Middle commuting triangle (blue) : This is an identity involving two left envelopes with the same codomain. The commutation follows from Lemma
5.1
(i) and Lemma
6.7
(i) .

4.

Lower commuting triangle (red) : This is an identity involving two right envelopes with the same codomain. The commutation follows from Lemma
5.1
(ii) and Lemma
6.7
(ii) .

This completes the proof of Theorem
4
. ∎

There are many spaces used commonly to establish various mathematical properties, but lacking a notion of ”full” or ”null”. Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
provide a means of filling these gaps. The examples discussed in the previous Section. It would require a pre-existing notion of nullity, as captured in (
6
), and a link as in (
10
) to the target category. As we have argued before, the notion of prevalence is a special case of the construction in (
13
). Although the former was done using classical Analysis, relying on a categorical approach offers many advantages. Firstly it provides a neat organization and separation of the various spaces and relations. Secondly the invariance of the resultant nullity structure with respect to morphisms in the category are automatically guaranteed by functoriality. Finally the categorical approach distills out the most essential features of the construction. For example in the case of Prevalence, properties such as reflexivity, or norm and inner product structure was non-essential.

Question 1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a sub-category of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
with full object set, and the former has a nullity structure
\Null:\mathcal{C}\to\Nullity
. Then how can one characterize the maximal sub-category of
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
that preserves the same nullity structure ?

In many practical scenarios a transformation presents itself as a morphism in a category
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
which is richer than a categorical structure
\mathcal{C}
. For example
(
BerryDas2023learning
, e.g.)
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
may be category of vector spaces with continuous nonlinear maps,
\mathcal{C}
equals
\left[\text{Vec}\right]
, and the morphism in question is a certain fixed point operator arising in the study of skew products such as (
2
). Then the challenge is to prove that the morphism preserves the notion of prevalence.
Once again, the categorical approach presented seems to be the most promising path to answering these questions.

6  Appendix

6.1  Functors induced between comma categories

Lemma 6.1
.

(
Das2024slice
, Prop 6)
Consider the arrangement of categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.4492,0.3906,0.1094}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5664,0.1484,0.6523}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}

(21)

Then there is an induced functor between comma categories

\Psi:\left[F\;;\;G\right]\to\left[F^{\prime}\;;\;G^{\prime}\right],

(22)

Moreover, the following commutation holds with the marginal functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[F\;;\;G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[F^{\prime}\;;\;G^{\prime}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{2}}

(23)

Lemma

6.2  Pre- and post- adjionts

Let
T:A\to B
be a functor. Then
T^{*}:B\to A
is said to be a
pre-right adjoint
to
T
if
TT^{*}\Rightarrow Id_{B}
. Such a functor
T
is said to be
pre-right adjoint enabled
or in brief pre-r.a.e.. Similarly,
T^{*}:B\to A
is said to be a
post-right adjoint
to
T
if
Id_{B}\Rightarrow TT^{*}
. In that case
T
will be called
post-right adjoint
enabled or in brief post-r.a.e..

Limits and co-limits

Suppose
J
and
\mathcal{X}
are two categories. Then for any object
x\in ob(\mathcal{X})
there is a constant functor that sends every object of
J
into
x
, and every morphism into
\Id_{x}
. This functor is denoted as
x:J\to\mathcal{X}
. Given a functor
F:J\to\mathcal{X}
, a
co-cone
for
F
is a natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow x
from
F
to a constant functor
x
. The object
x
is called the
tip
of the co-cone. A
colimit
of
F
is a universal co-cone, that means it is a co-cone
\bar{\eta}:F\Rightarrow\bar{x}
such that for any other co-cone
\eta:F\Rightarrow x
, there is a unique
\phi:\bar{x}\to x
such that
\eta=\phi\circ\bar{\eta}
. The tip
\bar{x}
of the universal co-cone is also called the colimit of the functor
F
. Colimits are closely related to their dual notion :
limits
.

Colimits and limits are a sweeping generalization of various fundamental constructs in mathematics, such as limits, convergence, min, max, union, intersection, inverse images, and gluing. The functor
J
can interpreted as a pattern and
F
as a diagram existing within
\mathcal{X}
in the shape of
J
.
J
may be finite or infinite. For example a terminal object is thus the colimit of the identity functor
\Id_{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
. The colimit
\bar{x}
provides a sense of completion or termination of the pattern. Colimits may not exist, but if they do, they are unique up to isomorphism.

The first technical result provides a sufficient condition under which pre-composition with a functor preserves limits and colimits.

Lemma 6.2
.

(
Das2023CatEntropy
, Lem 4.2)
Consider the following arrangement of functors and categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

1.

If both
\colim g
and
\colim fg
exist, and
f
has a post-right adjoint, then
\colim fg=\colim g
.

2.

If both
\lim g
and
\lim fg
exist, and
f
has a pre-right adjoint, then
\lim fg=\lim g
.

The next result shows how a left and right pre/post adjoint pair carries over to comma categories.

Lemma 6.3
.

Consider the following arrangement of functors and categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

By Lemma
6.1
there is an induced functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[fa\;;\;E\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[f\;;\;E\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}}

Then
a^{*}
above has a pre / post right adjoint if
a
has a pre / post-right adjoint.

The pre- or post right adjoint
a^{*}
leads to the following commuting diagrams that connect comma categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces aa^{8}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

The claim of the lemma now follows from the observation that there is a natural transformation between
aa^{*}
and
\Id_{B}
.

6.3  Kan extensions

Kan extensions are functors, and it is often possible to determine their action on objects, as shown below :

Lemma 6.4
.

Consider the arrangement of (
12
). Then

\begin{split}\mbox{If }E\mbox{ is cocoomplete, }\mbox{ then }&\mathcal{RE}_{K}\left(F\right)(d)=\colim\left\{Fx\;:\;Kx\to d\right\}\\
\mbox{If }E\mbox{ is coomplete, }\mbox{ then }&\mathcal{LE}_{K}\left(F\right)(d)=\lim\left\{Fx\;:\;d\to Kx\right\}\\
\end{split}

(26)

The colimit and limit in (
26
) are along slices of the object
d
along
K
. This construction is known as the
pointwise
definition of Kan extensions. Note that each slice, left or right, can be interpreted as a constraint on the objects of
K
.
If
E
is a co-complete category, the left Kan extension always exists. Similarly if
E
is a complete category, the right Kan extension always exists. In case both left and right Kan extensions of
F
along
K
exist, they combine to produce the following diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{RE}_{K}\left(F\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{LE}_{K}\left(F\right)}

(27)

We next present a series of lemmas that establish conditions under which Kan extensions are compatible with various pre and post compositions with functors.

Lemma 6.5
.

Consider the following arrangement of functors
a,b,f
as shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{LE}_{f}\left(b\right)}

1.

If
C
is co-complete and
a
has a post-right adjoint, then
\mathcal{RE}_{f}\left(b\right)=\mathcal{RE}_{fa}\left(ba\right)
.

2.

If
C
is complete and
a
has a pre-right adjoint, then
\mathcal{LE}_{f}\left(b\right)=\mathcal{LE}_{fa}\left(ba\right)
.

Proof.

We only prove Claim (i) as Claim (ii) is analogous. Fix an object
e\in E
. We have the following commutation diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[fa\;;\;e\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[f\;;\;e\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}^{(fa)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}^{(f)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

The commutation square follows from Lemma
6.1
, while the commutation triangle follows from construction. By Since
C
is co-complete, Lemma
6.4
applies and we have :

\mathcal{RE}_{fa}\left(ba\right)(e)=\colim ba\bot_{2}^{(fa)}=\colim b\bot_{2}^{(f)}a_{*}=\colim b\bot_{2}=\mathcal{RE}_{f}\left(b\right)(e).

The second equality follows from the commutative diagram, the third follows from Lemma
6.3
, and fourth and final equality follows again from Lemma
6.4
. This completes the proof of Lemma
6.5
.
∎

Consider the following arrangement of functors

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

1.

If
C
is complete and
e
has a pre right adjoint, then the commutation on the left holds, i.e.,
\mathcal{LE}_{ed}\left(c\right)\circ e=\mathcal{LE}_{d}\left(c\right)
.

2.

If
C
is co-complete and
e
has a post right adjoint, then the commutation on the right holds, i.e.,
\mathcal{RE}_{ed}\left(c\right)\circ e=\mathcal{RE}_{d}\left(c\right)
.

Proof.

We only prove Claim (ii), as Claim (i) is analogous. Since
C
is co-complete, it is sufficient to show that the two functors are equal pointwise. So fixing an arbitrary
x\in D
we have to show that
\mathcal{RE}_{ed}\left(c\right)(e(x))=\mathcal{RE}_{d}\left(c\right)(x)
. We have the following commutation diagram

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The projection of this induced functor along the first components leads to the following commuting diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[de\;;\;e(x)\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[d\;;\;x\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}^{(de)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot_{1}^{(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

Then we have

\mathcal{RE}_{ed}\left(c\right)(e(x))=\colim c\bot_{1}^{(de)}=\colim c\bot_{1}^{(d)}e_{*}=\colim c\bot_{1}=\mathcal{RE}_{d}\left(c\right)(x).

The first and fourth equalities follow from Lemma
6.4
, the second from the commutation. Since
e
has a post-right adjoint, so does
e_{*}
by Lemmas
6.3
. The third equality now follows from
6.2
(i). This completes the proof of Lemma
6.6
.
∎

1.

If
C
is complete and
e
and
a
have pre right adjoints, then the commutation on the left holds.

2.

If
C
is co-complete and
e
and
a
have post right adjoints, then the commutation on the right holds.

The commutation follows from :

\begin{split}\mathcal{RE}_{d}\left(ba\right)&=\mathcal{RE}_{ed}\left(ba\right)e\quad\mbox{ by Lemma \ref{lem:Kan_id:3}}~(ii),\\
&=\mathcal{RE}_{fa}\left(ba\right)e\\
&=\mathcal{RE}_{f}\left(b\right)e\quad\mbox{ by Lemma \ref{lem:Kan_id:2}}~(i).\end{split}

This completes the proof of Lemma
6.7
. ∎

6.4  Proof of (
20
)

The first commutation square comprises of comma categories and induced functors. The commutation follows from the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Set}\right\rrbracket}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Each of the four corners represent the constituents of the four comma categories involved. Lemma
6.1
applies to each of the tripes of functors to provide the final commutation between comma categories. Similarly, the second commutation square in (
20
) follows from the following deconstructive commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}

) is a direct application of (

). ∎

6.5  Proof of Lemma
5.1

Lemma
6.1
will be used to prove the right inverses of the induced functors between comma categories. This is done via the following diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Each row represents the constituents of a comma category. The triple of arrows from the second to the third row constitute
\pi_{*}
. The triple of arrows created by composition, from the first to the third row are identities. This makes the triple of arrows from the first to the second layer a right inverse to
\pi_{*}
. The right inverse of
\bot_{2}
follows in a similar manner from the following diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\star}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\mathcal{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0586,0.3672,0.0742}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Here
\star
denotes the trivial 1-point category. We have used the trivial fact that the comma category created out of
\star\rightarrow\star\leftarrow\mathcal{C}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}
. This completes the proof of Claim (i).

\iota_{1}
